God, the steward, bestower, and author of all good things, both visible and invisible: by your great power today, [order] my weak mind and direct my will toward your good pleasure. O best God and author, direct my mastery <note>original: "dominacionem," likely referring to the practitioner's command over the spirits</note> and, King of Kings, do not look upon the multitude of my sins, with which I, a wretch, am repeatedly covered; but through your immense mercy, bestow upon me your infinite grace.

Visit me with the visitation of the Holy Spirit, and let your piety <note>original: "pietas," implying both mercy and divine duty</note> have mercy upon all the stains of the flesh which I have contracted from sins; in your immense piety, wash them away and wipe them clean, lest my [sins] weigh me down—you who led lost man back to the pristine state of **innocence** <note>original: "innocencie"</note>. 

You, who by your **wisdom** <note>original: "sapiencia"; a reference to the Book of Wisdom 8:1: "She reacheth therefore from end to end mightily, and ordereth all things sweetly."</note> reach from one end to the other strongly and dispose all things sweetly: therefore, most merciful God, give to me, a wretched sinner, give to me in your works confirmation, perfection, accomplishment, and constancy...

I conjure you by his death and burial, by the mystery and power of the holy cross, by his **descent into hell** <term>harrowing of hell: the theological belief that Christ descended into the underworld to liberate the souls of the righteous</term>, by the power with which he conquered the infernal prison. 

I conjure you by his glorious resurrection and ascension, by the coming of the **Holy Spirit the Paraclete** <term>Paraclete: from the Greek 'parakletos,' meaning 'advocate' or 'helper,' referring to the Holy Spirit</term>, so that to me and kindly [you respond] as above <note>original: "ut s̄"; abbreviation for "ut supra," meaning to repeat the standard polite appearance formula</note>. I conjure you by the tremendous day of judgment. I conjure you by the day of eternal punishment, by the day of the misery of **Gehenna** <term>Gehenna: a valley near Jerusalem used as a metaphor for the place of fire and torment in the afterlife</term> and wretchedness, so that you obey my commands without delay, to me and kindly appearing <note>original: "pnt̄ īr"; likely "praesentando ire," to come and present yourselves</note> as above. 

I conjure you by **Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego** <note>The three youths in the Book of Daniel who were cast into a fiery furnace but remained unharmed</note>. I conjure you by the three magicians of **Pharaoh**, by the **Seal of Solomon** <term>Seal of Solomon: a legendary signet ring attributed to King Solomon, traditionally depicted as a hexagram or pentagram, which gave him the power to command demons</term>. I conjure you by the three **Magi** of Jesus Christ, namely **Caspar, Balthazar, and Melchior**, so that you come quickly. And I call you to witness by the command of the Lord that you come, that to me [you appear] kindly. 

Adjured by the Father, by the Son, by the **Holy Spirit**. 

Repeat these things a first time, and a second, then a third, and no more.

# Powder for the rheum <note>original: "Rei pulver." This likely refers to a powder used to treat "rheum"—the discharge of fluids from the eyes, nose, or joints.</note>

Also shall one keep the person <unclear>warm</unclear> without end, and if they wish to drink this until they are intoxicated, then one shall lay the person down on their right side. And one shall give [them] a drink, and of this drink [give] some small spoonful; so the [humor] goes to the outermost limbs on the right side and presses on the red [vessels] in front, so that the [strength] is restored. Now [take] bayberries and also [apply] with a soft cloth to the <unclear>head</unclear>.

Likewise: if the piles <term>marijs</term> <note>original: "marijs," from the Latin *marisca*, meaning hemorrhoids.</note> should fall out and a protrusion suffers therefrom: 

So one shall take the flowers of the sloe-thorn <note>original: "dorn landels blümen." Blackthorn or sloe flowers were commonly used as a purgative and tonic.</note> and one shall take the middle "tongue" [leaf] of the heather <unclear>heyde</unclear>, and the berries of juniper <term>Wacholdery</term>, and the middle shoots of juniper <term>Wacholdery</term> [summer-growth]. And one shall pound them into a powder, and one shall shave ivory <term>helfenbeine</term> <note>Ivory shavings were frequently used in medieval pharmacy, believed to have "cooling" properties and to strengthen the heart.</note> into it. 

One shall [put] a handful <term>hantff</term> of everything into a mortar <term>mortser</term> [to mix] one with another; one shall drink [this] with a [holy distillate]; so it heals also. Furthermore, when the "French disease" <note>original: "franz." ### Item: Concerning the disfigured pocks under the eyes
### as it is called "gorssia" <note>original: "gorssia." This term likely refers to *gorsia* or *gorsis*, a medieval medical term for a crusty or rough skin condition, often associated with the early stages of the "French disease" or syphilis.</note>

So shall one take the white of eggs <term>eyerviss</term> <note>Middle Low German *eyerviss* (Eierweiß).</note> from several eggs and shall mix it with rose water and shall make a plaster with linen cloth. Such a plaster draws out from the face all evil pocks; such a plaster one should [also] lay under the feet.

---

### Item: Another good purifying water for it

Take the red snails <term>rode snecken</term> and shall put them in a quart pot <term>dyppe</term> with the bile-full parts <term>galbvull</term> <note>Likely referring to the internal organs of the snails, which were believed to have medicinal properties.</note> and therein [add] three spoonfuls of salt and set it in the oven for two or three hours; thereafter put fat <term>smals</term> <note>Lard or rendered animal fat.</note> on it. Then one shall strain it through a cloth and put it in a glass vial <term>gotteroff</term> and set it in the sun for several days; so let it temper. This one shall smear under the eyes on the face or wherever it is necessary. When [the skin] is disfigured, then one shall wash it three [times] with distilled wine <term>gebrante wyn</term> <note>Literally "burnt wine," referring to brandy or aqua vitae used as a disinfectant.</note>.

---

### Item: Another good treatment <note>original: "proyke," likely a variant of *praktik* or *probe*, meaning a proven remedy.</note> for it

So shall one take <unclear>eal osad</unclear> and egg yolks <term>eyer dutery</term> from ten or twelve eggs and shall take frankincense <term>thymmīth</term> <note>A corruption of *thymiama*, a general term for incense or aromatic fumigants used in medicine.</note> and a spoonful of salt and pound that in a mortar and wring it through a cloth. Then take half a dram <term>quentīn</term> of powder of realgar <term>realit</term> <note>Realgar is an arsenic sulfide mineral. Despite its high toxicity, it was a standard historical treatment for stubborn skin lesions and syphilitic sores.</note> and take powder of "killed" mercury <term>getodt quecksilber</term> <note>"Killed" mercury refers to elemental mercury that has been processed—usually by grinding it with fat, saliva, or honey—until the shiny globules disappear, making it usable in an ointment.</note>. This shall all be tempered together, and when one sits in a bath, one shall smear oneself on the <unclear>sores</unclear> with it; thus it heals. It shall also [cause] the pocks to dry up and open, so that it passes away.

One may also well take one pound <note>original: "pud," likely a measure similar to a pound or a specific portion.</note> of silver litharge <term>silbergs</term> <note>Litharge is a lead oxide byproduct of silver refining, historically used in ointments to dry out sores and whiten the skin.</note> and pound that to a powder and put it into pure spring water <term>born</term>. It is good for the "up-thrown matter" <note>original: "ufschemasse," likely referring to discharge or phlegm.</note> and heals.

**Another good [remedy for] martagon** <term>martzene</term> <note>Likely referring to the Martagon lily or a specific skin condition named after it.</note>: then one shall extinguish thirty small pieces of powder of **couch-grass** <term>queckelēssē</term> <note>Also known as twitch-grass, historically used for its cooling and anti-inflammatory properties.</note> in the water, in the husks and leaves like **saffron**. If a woman’s **loins** <term>lende</term> <note>referring to the lower back or pelvic region.</note> should ache, then one shall take one **lot** <term>lot</term> <note>A historical unit of weight, approximately 14–16 grams or half an ounce.</note> of **liverwort** <term>leberwurtz</term> <note>Likely *Hepatica nobilis*, an herb traditionally used for the liver, but here applied to the lumbar region.</note> and shall boil it in three measures [of liquid]. When one is about to go to bed, one shall always drink the five spoonfuls of wine; after the third time, the **illness** <term>kranckheit</term> will pass away from her.

This is a **drink** <term>dranck</term> against **internal injury** <note>original: "ungelucke yn libe," literally "misfortune in the body," referring to internal trauma, hidden ailments, or poisoning.</note>, such as if a person has been struck or has suffered a **sudden sharp pain** <note>original: "schalt geschossen," referring to the folk-medical concept of being "shot" by a sudden ailment, often associated with "elf-shot" or sudden spasms.</note>, and the injury remains within the body, or for someone whose fat and flesh are swollen.

A person shall drink this, and it will drive [the ailment] out from the very depths.

A sickness called **cancer** <term>cancer</term> <note>In the medieval period, "cancer" (from the Latin for crab) referred to any persistent, spreading, or ulcerating skin lesion or sore that appeared to "eat" the flesh, rather than strictly the internal oncological disease known today.</note> is named after the **crab** <term>krebuß</term>; you should proceed and live [with it] just as the crab goes backward <note>original: "zu rugke gehn," suggesting the disease's retreating or unpredictable nature.</note>, and one shall cure it in this way:

One should first wash it with pure water; thus the **scabs** <term>schaltten</term> <note>Literally "scales" or "crusts" formed over a wound.</note> will soften.

Afterward, one shall make a **plaster** <term>plaster</term> in this way: for the mixture, one shall take **rue** <term>ruden</term>, **sorrel** <term>essigsauraung</term>, 1 **lot** <note>A historical unit of weight, roughly 15 grams or half an ounce.</note> of **verdigris** <term>Spangrun</term> <note>A green pigment/chemical made from weathered copper, used here for its powerful antibacterial properties.</note>, 1 lot of **alum** <term>Alaumte</term> <note>An astringent mineral used to stop bleeding and dry out wounds.</note>, 1 lot of **rock salt** <term>Salgemy</term>, 1 lot of **dragon's blood** <term>drachenblud</term> <note>A bright red resin obtained from certain trees, prized for its ability to "glue" wounds shut.</note>, 1 lot of **liver-colored aloe** <term>aloe epoticu</term> <note>Likely *Aloe hepatica*, a common medicinal grade of aloe resin.</note>, which is **burnt hartshorn** <term>gebrant hirschhorn</term> 1 lot <note>The text equates the aloe with burnt hartshorn, though they are different substances; possibly suggesting they are interchangeable or used in tandem.</note>, 1 lot of **red ochre** <term>rotry</term>, 1 lot of **pumice stone** <term>leynstein</term>, 1 lot of powder made from **burnt crab** <term>gebrant krebus</term> <note>An example of sympathetic medicine: using the ashes of the animal the disease is named after to cure it.</note>, 1 lot of **burnt human hair** <term>gebrante menschlicher har</term>, 1 lot of pure **frankincense** <term>weichrauch</term>, and 1 lot of <gloss>human</gloss> **gall** <term>gallen</term> <note>Bile or gall was often used in ointments to "cut through" corruption in a wound.</note>.

All of this shall be crushed in a mortar and rubbed well through a cloth. This powder shall be kept well so that it stays dry in the air. This shall then be rubbed into the wound. When the **cancer is dead**, test it thus: the wound heals itself with new skin all around and is not blue or pale around the edge.

---

**Another [remedy]**: one shall take the kernels from **hazelnuts** <term>haselnussen</term> and shall crush them and temper them with **honey** <term>honig</term> and then apply it as a plaster.

Also, for other **pustules** <term>pueste</term> <note>Inflamed, pus-filled sores or skin eruptions.</note> of **cancer** <term>krebus</term>, mix [crab] and salt and crush them together, and apply it thereon; so shall it [the cancer] die within [a few] days.

### Also for the stone, an exceedingly good remedy

So shall one take 3 **lots** <term>lot</term> <note>A "lot" is a historical unit of weight, roughly equivalent to 14–15 grams or half an ounce.</note> of **lovage** <term>louse ademyen</term> <note>Likely *Levisticum* (lovage), though the transcription "louse ademyen" is slightly obscured; lovage was a standard diuretic for breaking stones.</note>, 3 lots of **boxwood** <term>bissen pusboury</term> <note>Original: *pusboury* (Buchsbaum), often used in historical medicine for its purgative properties.</note>, and 3 lots of **parsley seed** <term>peterzilge same</term>. For the **swelling** <term>büly</term> <note>Likely a bubo or an inflammatory boil, often found in the groin or armpit.</note>, one shall take **blackberry** <term>bromen beer</term> herbs and roots and shall boil them with pure **spring water** <term>borne</term>, reducing it by three spans, boiling it in a pot until it is very soft and well-prepared. <meta>This page transitions from the spiritual and ritualistic healing of seizures or "possessions" described on the previous page to a specific physical remedy for a fistula—a chronic, tunnel-like sore or ulcer. The focus shifts from prayer and exorcism-like actions to herbal pharmacology and minor surgical intervention.</meta>

A **fistula drink** is to be made, which is good and has been truly tested, so that one may drive away **fistulas** <term>fisteln</term> <note>A fistula is an abnormal, infected passage forming in the body, often difficult to heal and requiring drainage.</note> and it also drives away the rough <unclear>bulge</unclear> <unclear>growth</unclear> <note>original: "Rau buck layne," likely referring to a protruding tumor or swelling.</note>, however powerful it may be.

So shall one take the root and leaves of **plantain** <term>Wegbreich</term> <note>Plantain (*Plantago*) was a staple of medieval wound medicine due to its ability to "draw" out infection and its astringent properties.</note>, the leaves of the **strawberry plant** <term>Erpbery</term>, the leaves of <unclear>adon</unclear>, the seed of **hemp** <term>hanff</term>, leaves of **mustard** <note>original: "senff"</note>, the uppermost part of **sharp radish** <note>original: "scharff lachich"</note>, a handful of **mallow** leaves <term>bappeln</term> <note>Mallow was used to soothe inflammation.</note>, **sundew** <note>original: "rousoli," likely *Ros solis*.</note>, and likewise **knotgrass** <term>vogel wesich</term> with the red flower; all of these are very good. And one shall boil them all well in **white wine** <note>original: "blanke Wein"</note> and water. Thereafter, one shall let it clarify. 

One shall give a very good portion of this to the patient. The drink shall be drunk morning and evening for as long as the medicine takes effect. However, as the fistula discharges, one should constantly hold it open with a **silver hook** <note>Silver was preferred for surgical tools in the Middle Ages because it was believed to stay "pure" and prevent further rot.</note>. And also constantly lay a **red cabbage leaf** upon it. 

Then one shall take the residue that remains from the <unclear>infusion</unclear> and one shall make a powder from it. And one shall take **camphor** and it shall be burned in a brass basin; one shall blow the powder into [the wound] with a **feather**, so it heals, as is written in number 83.

->It has been proven. Wherefore yet...<- <note>original: "Probatu est," a common Latin phrase in medical manuscripts meaning "it is tested/proven."</note>

<summary>This page provides a detailed herbal recipe and surgical instructions for treating a fistula using a medicinal wine, a silver hook for drainage, and a camphor-based powder. It demonstrates the transition from ritual healing to practical medieval pharmacology.</summary>
<keywords>fistula, herbal medicine, plantain, white wine, camphor, surgical tools, medieval pharmacy</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the physical medical treatments of the previous page (fistula remedies) to a judicial ritual. It describes the "Ordeal of Bread and Cheese" (iudicium panis et casei), a medieval practice used to identify a thief through a combination of liturgical prayer and a physical test of swallowing consecrated food.</meta>

### If you wish to know about a theft
### <unclear>and</unclear> to prove it

First, have a single loaf of bread made, like a small cake, and afterwards write this charm upon the bread—just like a little cake: "May God <unclear>curse</unclear> him; he is full of bitterness and deceit, under his tongue are labor and sorrow. Judge me, O God, and distinguish my cause." <note>original: "deus maladicat eo plenū est amaritudine et dolo sub lingua eius labor et dolor iudica me deus et discerne causam meā" — This inscription draws from Psalm 10:7 and Psalm 43:1, turning scripture into a protective or judicial formula.</note> 

Afterward, take a portion of cheese and write this charm upon that cheese: "The Lord shall know; let Him perform <unclear>your</unclear> judgment through my hand, and then [behold] he who is the sinner." <note>original: "Cognoscet dno iudicā faciat tuā mē manuā suā et tūc qui est peccator"</note> 

Once this is done, let him say this <unclear>prayer</unclear> four times over the bread and cheese: 

"God of dominions, God of virtues, God of angels and archangels, God of patriarchs and prophets, God of apostles and martyrs, God of confessors, virgins, and widows, God of all saints and blessed ones, God of all my faithful. Through this performance, so that the <term>truth</term> <note>original: "vray," likely from the Old French for "true," used here to mean the truth of the matter.</note> may be revealed, [use] your power so that he may vomit it back up before [swallowing]. For then the Lord God is the judge. Let it be done so, O Lord, according to your great mercy, so that the sin of the devil may be overcome; let him who is guilty in this just cause accept this bread and cheese, but let him not swallow it. Instead, let him vomit it back up. 

In this performance, let a trembling appear in the truth of it; let him shake and become like a <term>quivering tree</term> <note>original: "arbor tremulat," likely a reference to the aspen tree, which was a common medieval metaphor for guilt-induced shaking.</note>, and in his trembling let him have no [rest]. 

God of Abraham, God of Isaac, God of Jacob, God of Elijah and Tobias, God who delivered the three youths from the furnace of burning fire, etc." <note>This concluding invocation appeals to the "God of Deliverance," citing the biblical story of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego to emphasize God’s power to intervene in physical trials.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the ritual for the "Ordeal of Bread and Cheese," a form of trial by ordeal used to identify a thief by making them swallow inscribed food while reciting prayers; failure to swallow or vomiting the food was seen as proof of guilt.</summary>
<keywords>ordeal, trial by bread and cheese, theft, judicial ritual, medieval law, exorcism, liturgical prayer, biblical invocations</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the specific "experiment" of hydromancy (scrying with water) into a formal prologue for a larger collection of rituals. It establishes the spiritual requirements—faith, purity, and prayer—necessary for the magic to work, framing these practices within a Christian context.</meta>

# Here begins the prologue of the book for whoever [seeks] the ways, or for those things that have been discovered, and what they are by causes and effect
For whoever loves the work he begins and wishes to bring it to a conclusion, so that he may reach the desired outcome, let him prudently take precautions; he knows through himself what things are necessary, lest he [fail] in hope and wisdom. In the time of this instruction, which perfects others who are unskilled, it is both laudable and most right—as was [given] to our saints and apostles and brought to completion—so that no one might be able to be deceived in the work through it. Let him have his whole faith in Christ, from whom all good things proceed, just as the Lord says through the prophet: "He comes before [us]," and the faithful person cannot fail to be entirely saved. 

If, therefore, it is necessary for every worker to approach [God] so that faith does not fail, then for the one perfected in work and understanding, the error of certain unsuccessful ones <note>original: "effunctorum," likely meaning those whose efforts have "run out" or failed, or perhaps "the deceased."</note> must be removed so that he may succeed. Let him invoke the name of God and the saints, and through the ineffable Holy Spirit, and with truth and faith, he might effectively obtain what he ought to obtain, as will be declared below.

In this very host, He divided His virtue and power; but nevertheless, in the manner the prophet shall teach, it cannot be done [otherwise]. For there are many great things perfected through prayers and from day to day <note>original: "edue dadie," likely a corruption of "de die in diem."</note> they [strive to] reach the effect, yet they do not reach it, but instead they deceive themselves because they lose their ground. It is better to act with the brevity of a single word than to be led through arduous prayers. Whence it is said: "A prayer is better than much power." <note>This reflects a common medieval sentiment that the spiritual quality of a prayer was more important than the length or the raw power of the magician.</note> 

Thus, this petitioner avails more by prayer than [others], and all things finished are mixed both in body and for their own sake. Therefore, let the worker be diligent and prudently take precautions to the depths of his memory; afterward, wishing to work, let him scrutinize himself as to what he ought to do and what might be of value to him, lest he be deceived.

Therefore, as mentioned above, although this work has been made which is called the *Book of Prayers* <term>liber precationum</term>, the practitioner ought to abstain from all pollution of mind and body, as is fitting; and he should be moderate in his work, his sight, and his leisure. Then, let him clothe himself in clean garments, and so it is said that he should begin the work; and he must hear one Mass every day.

<summary>This prologue introduces a "Book of Prayers," framing ritual magic as a pious Christian endeavor that requires the practitioner to maintain physical and spiritual purity. It emphasizes that faith in Christ and the brevity of sincere prayer are more effective than long, complex rituals.</summary>
<keywords>prologue, Book of Prayers, spiritual purity, faith in Christ, ritual preparation, Mass, mental pollution, effectiveness of prayer</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the practitioner's spiritual preparation, moving from general faith to the specific physical and ritual treatment of the magical book itself. This book must be fashioned from good material until the Mass is finished, and he should do this most devoutly with devotion and fasting, just as it is written. Fasting and many prayers reveal heavenly mysteries and prepare for the performance of divine secrets. Daily after Mass, he should carry the book home and have a secret place removed from all others, in which he may remain bare <note>original: "nudus." In ritual magic, this can signify literal nakedness to represent purity or "bareness" of the soul, though it may also mean being solitary/uncovered.</note>, nor should anyone be present who might disturb the work. 

Let him sprinkle the place with holy water <term>aqua benedicta: water blessed by a priest, used here to purify the ritual space from demonic influence</term>. Where he places his book, let him bind the book with a precious girdle and a blessed stole <note>A liturgical vestment worn by deacons and priests; its use here suggests the magician is adopting a quasi-priestly role.</note> with twelve knots all around. On bended knees, let him place the book itself, from which he should recite the Seven Psalms <term>VII psalmos: the Seven Penitential Psalms (6, 32, 38, 51, 102, 130, and 143), traditionally used to express contrition.</term> with a long prayer and perform a litany <term>letania: a repetitive form of prayer consisting of a series of petitions.</term>. Let him bless and consecrate this book of his with these designated words, with all devotion and the full affection of his heart. Giving himself to the work in its truth and its way, let him know that the book is blessed and consecrated by its written and inscribed words, so that by the power it ought to obtain—namely, for the hope of its specific binding <note>Referring to the power to "bind" spirits.</note>—it may be effective for all invocations and conjurations of them, and for all other experiments. 

But let anyone who pollutes this book know that he will have no power here, unless he perseveres entirely without vice. Then, through prayer and litany, he brings the written book to life <note>original: "viuificat," literally "vivifies" or "gives life to."</note> and speaks through prayer with a humble heart and a devoted soul; then the psalm is spoken:

Hear, O God, the prayer of your servant, so that on this day it may be effective for the one invoking you. Hear me just as you heard Mary, your mother. Receive, Lord, the moisture <note>original: "humore," perhaps a metaphor for tears or a scribal error for "honore" (honor).</note> of my prayer; hear, O Lord, let my prayers be pleasing. May the prayer of the most blessed Virgin Mary, your mother, and of all the saints, and my prayer and my face be helped by your piety, which I, a sinner, pour out before you according to the operation in this hour... [in] the worst hour <note>Likely referring to the "evil hours" or astrologically difficult times mentioned elsewhere in the text.</note> which are contained <note>original: "glinent," a corruption of "continentur."</note> in this book. Through your prayer and grace, may it grant all he has desired, through our Lord Jesus Christ, who lives and reigns...

May He hear this conjuration through that holy one to whom is praise, honor, and glory through infinite ages of ages. Amen.

<note>This section lists the "Egyptian Days" (*dies Aegyptiaci*), which were considered lucky or unlucky days in the medieval calendar for various activities like bloodletting, traveling, or performing magic.</note>

### On the Metal of Tin and on the Stones of *Albirazi*, that is, Crystal
Concerning the animals: **rams**, **sheep**, and **calves**. Concerning the **pure herbs**. And make the ring on [his] day, and perfume <note>original: "subfinga"; likely a corruption of "suffumiga" (to fumigate/perfume with smoke)</note> it with **aloe** and **mastic**. <note>Aloe and mastic were expensive resins used to create "sweet" smoke pleasing to the benevolent planet Jupiter.</note>

And then Jupiter knows [how] to lead toward **favor** <note>original: "gratia"; this refers to social standing, charisma, and the kindness of superiors</note>, always as he speaks to anyone, he receives whatever he wants. And so it must be done in the mass of the one who disposed it for you. You shall conquer [with] the ring and what is said; and by the fear of what [his] power has done toward the **firmament**. Likewise, it ought to be performed in the virtue of the **Name** which is written for Jupiter, which should be most ready.

Concerning foxes <note>original: "vulpibus"</note>, as it is said, in the month of April during the full moon, one should remain [watchful]. Likewise, concerning the heart: then take care and dry it, and make a powder. Give it in food or in drink—a little, clean amount—to whom you wish and love, whether it be a man or a woman. Both are pricked <note>affected by desire or influence</note> just as when [approaching] puberty, or for that reason alone.

Likewise, when you wish to go before him, at that time well anoint your face, and you shall have from him that which you desire in a way that will be marveled at.

In the heavens, as the Moon travels through [her] mansions, she has a certain power, and in another mansion, she has another power.
